O|4|A day or two here and there
O|3|A week or so, around the bigger events
O|2|Most of the month, something is always coming
O|1|Practically all of it, I'm always waiting for the next thing
B|0|8|Dread is arriving early|The waiting is costing you more than the events themselves, and there's almost always something on the horizon. That is a heavy way to spend a month, and many people live this way without naming it. One small step: for the next thing you're dreading, write your prediction beforehand and what actually happened afterward. A therapist can help you use what you find.
B|9|12|The lead-up costs you|You get through the things you dread, but the days before them are shadowed and the nights are short. That is a common pattern. Try giving the dread a fixed appointment, ten minutes in the evening to think it over, and gently returning to the present when it shows up outside that slot.
B|13|16|A little nervous, then fine|You feel some dread ahead of events, but it stays in proportion and mostly shows up the day before. That's healthy nerves doing their job. Notice which kinds of events pull the dread forward, and try preparing those a little earlier so the waiting has less to feed on.
B|17|20|You wait lightly|You prepare, set it aside, and sleep. Events arrive without weeks of dread ahead of them. That ease is worth protecting, since it tends to fade first when life gets crowded. If you notice a knot in your stomach starting to arrive earlier than usual, take it as a signal to slow down.
Q|safety-behaviors|Your Safety Behaviors|Safety behaviors are the small things you do to feel okay in an anxious moment: sitting near the exit, holding your phone, keeping a script ready. This check-in asks how many you carry and how much they hold you up.|Anxiety,CBT,Habits|2
P|You're going somewhere new with people you don't know well. What do you bring “just in case”?
O|4|Nothing special
O|3|My phone, and I make sure I know where the exit is
O|2|A planned excuse to leave early and a friend on standby
O|1|A whole kit: excuse, exit, water, pills, and someone I can call
P|In a conversation that makes you anxious, where do your hands and eyes go?
O|4|Wherever they naturally go, I'm not thinking about it
O|3|I might fidget a bit, then relax
O|2|I grip my cup or phone and look at anything but their face
O|1|I hide behind something and count the seconds until it's over
P|If you had to do an anxious task without your usual props, like your phone or a supportive person, how would that feel?
O|4|Fine, I'd manage
O|3|A little exposed, but doable
O|2|Very hard, I'd probably put it off
O|1|Impossible, I wouldn't go
P|Think of a situation you get through only with a specific routine or ritual. How often does that come up?
O|4|Never, I don't have one
O|3|Maybe one situation, like flying
O|2|Several situations a week
O|1|Most of my day is built around them

O|3|Mostly myself, with a little luck
O|2|The props and people I leaned on
O|1|Pure luck, and next time it might not work
B|0|8|Held up by props|You've built a careful system of exits, scripts, and standbys, and it takes real effort to keep it running. It makes sense: those things have helped you get through. The catch is that they also keep you from finding out you could manage without them. One small step: drop a single prop in a low-stakes situation and see what happens. A therapist can help you plan those experiments.
B|9|12|Leaning on your toolkit|You get through anxious moments, but you credit the props more than yourself, and without them you'd hesitate. That's a very common place to be. Try noticing, after the next hard thing, which parts you actually handled on your own. Write them down. The list is usually longer than expected.
B|13|16|Mostly standing on your own|You keep a prop or two for the hardest situations, and otherwise you trust yourself in the room. That balance is healthy. The one or two rituals you still lean on are worth a curious look: what do you expect would happen without them? Testing that gently, once, can be freeing.
B|17|20|You trust yourself in the room|You walk into new situations without a safety kit and credit yourself when they go well. That confidence has probably been earned over time. Keep it by noticing if a stressful season brings any old props back, and be kind to yourself if it does. That's a signal, not a setback.
Q|catastrophizing-check|Catastrophizing Check|When a small thing goes wrong, how fast does your mind get to the worst case? This check-in looks at how far your thoughts jump and how easily you can bring them back down to size.|Thinking Traps,CBT,Anxiety|2
P|Your boss schedules a meeting with you and doesn't say why. Your mind goes to…
O|4|“Probably a project update”
O|3|“Hope it's nothing bad,” then I get on with work
O|2|“I'm in trouble,” and I check everything I've done lately
O|1|“I'm getting fired,” and I start mentally packing my desk
P|A minor headache turns up on a busy day. Within a few minutes, how big has it become in your head?
O|4|Just a headache, I take something and move on
O|3|A slight “hope it isn't something more” before it fades
O|2|A worry I keep returning to all afternoon
O|1|A brain tumor, and I'm scared to look it up
P|When you catch yourself imagining the worst, what happens next?
O|4|I ask myself what's actually likely and it deflates
O|3|I can talk myself down, though it takes a minute
O|2|I try to argue with it but the worst case keeps winning
O|1|I can't argue, the worst case feels like the only true one
P|A friend hears you describe a setback. What are they most likely to say?
O|4|“Sounds annoying, you'll be fine”
O|3|“I think it's smaller than it feels right now”
O|2|“You're jumping ahead again”
O|1|“You always think it's the end of the world”

O|2|One or two, which felt like proof
O|1|I've stopped counting the predictions, there are too many
B|0|8|Worst case feels certain|Your mind is jumping to the worst ending fast and staying there, and arguing with it doesn't seem to work. That is exhausting, and it's one of the most common thinking habits there is. One small step: when a worst case lands, write down the best case and the most likely case next to it, even if you don't believe them yet. A therapist can help you build that muscle.
B|9|12|The leap comes easy|You jump ahead often, and it takes real effort to come back. You usually manage it, but not before it has cost you an afternoon. That's familiar to many people. Try keeping a short record of predictions and outcomes for two weeks. Seeing the gap on paper is oddly reassuring.
B|13|16|You catch the leap|Your thoughts sometimes leap to the worst, but you notice and bring them back to size fairly quickly. That's a skill worth keeping sharp. Notice which areas of life the leaps cluster in, whether health, work, or relationships. That's where a little extra practice pays off.
B|17|20|Sized about right|You tend to see setbacks at their actual size and rarely spin them into disasters. That steadiness is real, and it likely calms the people around you too. Keep an eye on it during high-stress stretches, when even steady minds can start reaching for the worst case.
Q|nervous-system-settling|How Your Nervous System Settles|After stress spikes, your body is supposed to come back down. This check-in looks at how quickly and fully yours settles and what helps or gets in the way of that return.|Grounding,Anxiety,Rest|2
P|You've just had a tense conversation. Twenty minutes later, your body is…
O|4|Back to normal, maybe a little tired
O|3|Mostly calm, with some leftover buzz
O|2|Still tight, heart still quick
O|1|Just as keyed up as during the conversation
P|What does “winding down” actually look like for you most evenings?
O|4|A routine that reliably works, like a walk or a book
O|3|Scrolling or TV, which mostly does the job
O|2|Trying several things and nothing quite lands
O|1|I don't wind down, I just eventually pass out
P|When you're resting on a day off, how does your body behave?
O|4|It rests, I can feel myself soften
O|3|It takes a few hours, then I ease into it
O|2|It stays on alert, I keep waiting for something to do
O|1|It can't rest at all, doing nothing makes me more anxious
P|How often in the past month have you noticed you were holding your breath or breathing high in your chest?
O|4|Rarely, my breathing is mostly easy
O|3|A few times, and I slowed it down
O|2|Often, usually when I was already stressed
O|1|All the time, I can't remember breathing easily

O|2|“It takes you a long time to come down”
O|1|“You never really seem to come down”
B|0|8|Stuck in high gear|Your body seems to stay switched on long after the stress has passed, and rest itself can feel uncomfortable. That is a common state after a long stretch of pressure, and it can shift. One small step: before bed tonight, spend two minutes making your out-breath longer than your in-breath. A therapist can help you find more ways to bring your body down.
B|9|12|A slow return|You do come down, but it takes longer than you'd like and it needs the right conditions. Many people are in this spot. Try picking one small settling habit, like a short walk after work or a stretch before bed, and doing it at the same time every day for two weeks so your body learns to expect it.
B|13|16|You find your way down|You have things that help you settle, and they usually work within a reasonable time. Notice what's different on the days they don't: too much caffeine, too little sleep, a screen too late? A quick note can show you which lever to reach for first.
B|17|20|Your body knows the way back|Your body settles quickly after stress and rests fully when given the chance. That kind of recovery is worth protecting. Keep the routines that make it possible, and notice early if a busy season starts to shorten your wind-down. That's when it matters most.

B|13|16|You can stay a while|You can sit with discomfort for a while and let it pass, and you mostly don't let it run your week. That's a real skill. B|13|16|Loops with an exit|You get caught in a replay now and then, but you can name it and move on before it costs you much. That's a good place to be. B|17|20|You let it go|You review what happened, learn what you can, and let it fade. That's how reflection is supposed to work, and it's rarer than you might think. Keep it going, and if you know someone who gets stuck in loops, your way of talking to yourself could be worth sharing.

O|3|A couple
O|2|Most of them

P|When a worry catches you at midnight, what do you do with it?

O|3|I turn it over a bit, then manage to set it down

B|0|8|The mind takes the night shift|Your thoughts get loudest exactly when you need them quietest, and lying down has become something to brace for. That is exhausting, and it is very common. Try a worry list on paper an hour before bed, written and closed, so the thoughts have somewhere to live. If nights stay this hard, a therapist can help you work with the pattern.
B|9|12|Loud, then slowly quieter|Your mind runs at bedtime, though you can usually bring it down with effort. That effort is worth spending earlier. Give yourself ten minutes of planned thinking time in the early evening, and notice whether the nights get lighter once the day has a place to land.
B|13|16|Thoughts pass through|Some nights are busier than others, but your thoughts mostly come and go without taking over. Notice which days feed the louder nights, whether it is late work or a hard conversation, and give those days an extra bit of wind-down.
B|17|20|Quiet in the dark|Your mind settles when the room does, and worries mostly know to wait until morning. That is a skill many people are still building, and it is worth appreciating. Keep the habits that support it, and be patient with yourself on the rare wired night.

B|9|12|Screens have a foothold|You know the screen keeps you up, and some nights it wins. A gentle line helps more than a hard rule: choose a stop time for scrolling, thirty minutes before bed, and put something you actually enjoy in its place.
B|13|16|Mostly in your hands|Screens are part of your evening but they are not in charge. The occasional binge is normal. Notice what you reach for at 3 a.m., and if it is your phone, try a plain clock by the bed so the phone can sleep elsewhere.
B|17|20|Nights belong to you|You have kept screens out of the space where sleep happens, and your rest shows it. That takes real intention in a world designed for the opposite. Keep the boundary friendly rather than strict, and share what helped if a friend is struggling with the same pull.

B|9|12|Rest comes with guilt|You do rest, though it tends to arrive late and with a side of guilt. The guilt is the part to work on. Notice the thought that shows up when you sit down, and write it out once so you can see how harsh it sounds on paper.
B|13|16|Pausing on purpose, mostly|You choose rest more often than it chooses you, and your body thanks you for it. Keep an eye on the busy seasons when the pauses quietly disappear, and decide in advance what the smallest version of rest looks like for those weeks.
B|17|20|Rest is part of the plan|You stop before you are empty, and you treat rest as fuel rather than a reward. That is a hard-won balance. Keep it, and if someone near you is heading for the wall, modeling this quietly may do more than any advice.

B|9|12|Hearing it late|You catch your body’s signals, but often once they have gotten loud. That is workable. Pick one signal you tend to miss, such as thirst or tension, and set a gentle midday reminder to check on it before it has to shout.
B|13|16|A working conversation|You and your body mostly keep in touch, and you act on what you hear more often than not. On the days you go quiet, notice what pulled you away. A short note in a journal about what your body needed that day can sharpen the listening.
B|17|20|In close touch|You notice what your body needs and you respond to it without much fuss. That is a form of self-respect that took time to build. Keep it, especially in the seasons when everyone else’s needs get louder than your own.

B|0|8|The basics have slipped|Water, food, sleep and air have been falling off the list, and it is probably not because you don’t know they matter. B|13|16|A little give|Your weekends stretch a bit and your body copes, with a mildly slow Monday. That is a normal amount of flex. Notice the weekends where the drift grows, and treat a big shift as a sign that the week left you more depleted than you realized.
B|17|20|Steady all week|Your sleep barely changes between weekdays and weekends, and Monday is just a day. That steadiness is a real gift to your body and your mood. Keep it without becoming rigid about it, and if a friend dreads every Monday, a gentle word about wake times may help.

B|0|8|Obligation runs the calendar|Your social life seems to be made of things you did not choose, and the only exits you can picture are illness or a cancellation. B|13|16|Mostly chosen|Most of what you show up for, you chose, and you can bow out of things that have stopped working. That is a good balance. Notice the recurring event or the person whose invitations still feel like a summons, and decide whether that is a limit you want to set.
B|17|20|You go because you want to|You accept what you want, decline what you don’t, and stay warm while you do it. That keeps your yes meaningful. B|17|20|Practiced, not perfect|You expect boundaries to wobble now and then, repair them without shame, and can even tell the other person you are changing course. That is mature and rare. O|3|That I manage, though I run a little ragged
O|2|That I lean hard on one or two habits that worry them
O|1|That I disappear, or that I fall apart
B|0|8|Toolkit nearly empty|Getting through hard moments seems to depend on luck or on things that cost you later. That is exhausting, and it is not a character flaw; nobody handed you the tools. Pick one small, gentle thing, like a five-minute walk or a text to someone kind, and practice it on an ordinary day so it is ready when you need it. A therapist can help you build the rest.
B|9|12|A few tools, some worn out|You have some ways to cope, but a couple of them may be leaving you worse off, and there is not much backup. That is a very normal place to be. Try writing a short list of what helps and what only feels like it helps, then add one new item to the first column.
B|13|16|A solid, working set|You have several ways to get through a hard patch and mostly choose them on purpose. That is real skill. Keep an eye on the one habit that sneaks in when you are tired, and make sure your best tools are easy to reach on your worst days.
B|17|20|Well stocked and flexible|You have a range of ways to cope, you match them to the moment, and they leave you better off afterward. That takes practice and self-knowledge. Stay curious about whether any tool is becoming automatic rather than chosen, and consider sharing what works with someone who is struggling.

O|3|A few times, when I was very tired

B|9|12|Checking out too often|Shutting down is a regular part of how you weather stress, and it costs you time, connection and clarity. Many people who never learned to fight or flee learned to freeze instead. Practice saying “I need a minute” before you go blank, even if only in your head.
B|13|16|Quiet, but still here|You get quiet under pressure, yet you mostly stay reachable and recover reasonably fast. That is a workable rhythm. Tell the people close to you what your quiet means and how they can help, so it never reads as rejection.
B|17|20|Present through the storm|You tend to stay connected and thinking even when stress is high, and you know how to come back if you drift. That is genuinely steady. Guard it by resting before you run empty, and be patient with people whose stress makes them disappear.

O|3|Like pressure that eases once I get moving
O|2|Like an itch I have to scratch right now
O|1|Like a command I cannot say no to

B|13|16|Riding most waves|You usually notice an urge, let it rise, and choose what to do. That is real skill, not luck. Notice which conditions, like tiredness or loneliness, shrink your gap, and keep your favorite “wait it out” tool close on those days.
B|17|20|Waves come and go|You seem to treat urges as passing weather rather than orders, and you have a few reliable ways to wait them out. That is a well-built skill. Keep it sharp by staying honest about the rare urge that still wins, and share the wave idea with someone who might need it.

B|9|12|Mostly fine, not really fine|You share some of what you feel but smooth over a lot, and “I’m fine” is doing heavy lifting. That is very common. Once a day, try answering “how are you?” with one true word instead of the automatic one, and see how it lands.
B|13|16|Honest, with a light filter|You mostly show what you feel, with a bit of tact that keeps things smooth. That is a healthy balance. Notice the one feeling you still tend to hide, often anger or need, and see whether it could be said to someone who would handle it well.
B|17|20|What you feel, they see|You tell the truth about your feelings, kindly and soon, and your relationships are stronger for it. That is real courage made ordinary. Keep checking that honesty stays paired with care for the listener, and make room for people who are still learning to say what they feel.

Q|distress-tolerance|Distress Tolerance|Looks at how you get through moments that are painful and cannot be fixed right away, without making them worse. Uses ideas from DBT in plain language, for anyone who struggles to sit through a hard hour.|DBT,Grounding|2

O|3|“Hang on, it will pass eventually”

B|0|8|Hard moments become harder|When something painful cannot be fixed right away, the urge to escape seems to take over and leave you worse off. B|9|12|Getting through, at a cost|You survive the unfixable moments, but frazzled and sometimes with a decision you regret. That is a common starting point. Write a short list of three things that help your body settle, keep it on your phone, and try the first one before the peak, not after.
B|13|16|Steady enough to wait|You can mostly sit through painful stretches without making them worse, and you have some tools that work. That is real skill. Pay attention to the moments that still tip you, often when tired or alone, and add one tool specifically for those.
B|17|20|Weathering it well|You get through hard hours with steadiness and skill, and your choices in those moments tend to hold up afterward. That took practice. Keep it sharp by staying honest about the rare time it slips, and consider sharing what helps with someone who is in the middle of it.
Q|acting-opposite-to-the-urge|Acting Opposite to the Urge|Explores whether you can do the reverse of what a feeling demands, like moving when sadness says stay still or staying when fear says run. A DBT idea in plain language, for anyone stuck in the same loops.|DBT,Emotion Regulation|2

O|2|I cancel, and feel worse for it

B|0|8|Feelings calling every shot|Right now the urge that comes with a feeling tends to win, whether that is staying in bed, avoiding or lashing out, and your life may have narrowed as a result. B|9|12|Going along with the urge|You sometimes push against what a feeling demands, but more often you follow it and pay later. That is where most people begin. Pick one feeling and one opposite action that fits it, then commit to trying it just once before the week is out.
B|13|16|Pushing back when it matters|You can act against a feeling’s pull when it counts, and you have felt the payoff. That is real skill. Look for the one feeling you still tend to obey, since most people have one, and build a small opposite action for it.
B|17|20|Choosing, not obeying|You reliably check whether a feeling’s advice fits the facts and act on what is good for you, even when it is uncomfortable. That is a strong, practiced skill. Remember that sometimes the feeling is right and worth following, and be gentle with people who are still learning the difference.

B|0|8|Grief is everywhere right now|Grief is taking up nearly everything, and that is a real, human response to losing someone who mattered. There is no clock on this and no right way to do it. B|9|12|Carrying it day by day|You are getting through, and it is costing you. Grief this heavy is not a sign you are doing it wrong; it is a sign of how much you loved. Try letting one of the basics, a meal or a walk, be non-negotiable this week. Talking with a therapist can help you carry it.
B|13|16|Waves, with ground between them|Grief still arrives in waves, and between them you have real footing. Both things are true at once, and that is what living with loss often looks like. Keep the people who know the real version close, and consider writing a few lines to the person you lost when a wave passes.
B|17|20|Grief has its own room|Your grief has room to visit without running the day, and you are tending to yourself and letting others in. That is hard-won. It can still surge without warning, so keep the door open rather than deciding you are done. If someone else you know is grieving, what you have learned may be a gift to them.

B|0|8|Still in the deep water|Life after this loss feels frozen or hollow, and that can be true whether it has been six months or six years. Grief keeps its own calendar. One gentle next step: pick a single small thing they loved and do it this week, in their honor, with someone beside you. If it ever feels like too much, in the US you can call or text 988 at any time.
B|9|12|Moving, but heavily|You are moving through life, and each part of it takes more out of you than people can see. That is common, and it is not a failure to move on. Try naming one invitation or task you have been dodging and take the smallest piece of it. A therapist who knows grief can be a steady companion here.
B|13|16|Sad and living, both|You are living your life with the loss woven into it rather than walling it off. Sadness still turns up, and you let it. Keep telling the stories when their name comes up; that is one of the ways love keeps its shape after someone is gone.
B|17|20|Carrying them forward|You have made room for a life that holds the person you lost without being ruled by the loss. That took time and courage. Waves may still come at anniversaries or out of nowhere, and that is not a step backward. Consider writing down what you have learned about grieving; it may help you or someone else later.

B|0|8|Frozen at the edge|The change feels enormous and you are stalled at the edge, which is a normal place to stand when the stakes are high. Nothing needs deciding today. Try writing the change at the top of a page and listing what you would need to know before you could choose. That turns fear into questions.
B|9|12|Wanting it, bracing for it|Part of you is pulling toward this and part of you is bracing hard. Both parts deserve a hearing. Choose one person you trust and say the plan out loud, including the doubts. Hearing yourself explain it often shows you what you actually think.
B|13|16|Leaning in, feet still planted|You know why you want this and you have started to move, with some hesitation still holding you in place. That is a reasonable pace, not a delay. Sketch a rough fallback plan this week; knowing you could recover often frees the last bit of hesitation.
B|17|20|Ready, with eyes open|You have done the thinking, taken real steps and made peace with the chance it goes wrong. That is what readiness looks like. Set a date for the next step so momentum does not fade, and keep one steady person in the loop as you go. Big changes are easier with a witness.

B|9|12|Looking for the thread|There are threads of meaning in your life, but they are buried under obligation and hard to find on a tired day. Try a short daily note answering one question: what did I do today that felt like me? Two weeks of answers will tell you a lot.

B|17|20|Living with a why|Your days connect to something you value, and you can name it. That is a real resource, especially when life gets hard. Meaning shifts as we change, so revisit the question now and then. Telling someone younger what has given your life its shape is a generous way to keep it alive.

B|13|16|Mostly in step|Your choices mostly track what you care about, with some comfort-driven detours. That is a good place to be. When a value and comfort clash next, pause for one breath before choosing. The pause is often enough to tip it.
B|17|20|Walking your talk|What you value and how you live match up, and the people around you can see it. That kind of alignment is steadying for everyone near you. Values evolve, so check the list once or twice a year and let it change. Consider talking about yours with someone who is still working theirs out.

B|0|8|Regret is running the show|Looking back has become a loop that eats your present and turns every old choice into evidence against you. That is a painful way to live, and it is common. Try this: write the regret as if a friend had done it, and answer them. Talking with a therapist about self-blame can loosen it further.
B|9|12|Looking back too long|Regret gets a lot of your attention, especially late at night, and the lesson has not yet turned into much relief. Pick one regret and write down what you would do differently now. Then do one small thing that proves it, this week.
B|13|16|Learning from it|You look back with some sting and mostly pull something useful from it. That is what a healthy relationship with regret looks like. When the apologizing creeps in as you tell a story, notice it and try telling the story once without it.
B|17|20|At peace with the past|You can hold your past choices with kindness and a bit of humor, and you have turned regret into something you actually live by. That is rare and worth protecting. Keep an eye on new regrets forming, and offer your younger-self tone to anyone around you who is stuck in theirs.

O|2|Decide I was right to be afraid
O|1|Never try that thing, or anything like it, again
P|In small everyday choices, ordering something new, speaking up in a group, you…
O|4|Lean toward the unfamiliar more often than not
O|3|Mix it up, depending on my energy
O|2|Play it safe nearly every time
O|1|Choose whatever draws the least attention
B|0|8|Fear is deciding for you|Fear has been making most of your choices, and the things you are avoiding have started to pile up. That is exhausting, and it is not a character flaw; it is a pattern, and patterns can change. Pick the smallest scary thing on your list, something that takes under five minutes, and do it this week. Nothing bigger yet.
B|9|12|Playing it safe|You feel the fear and it usually wins, though not always. That is a common place to be. Try a tiny daily act of courage, such as one sentence in a meeting or one new dish, and keep a simple tally. Courage is a muscle, and small reps count.
B|13|16|Brave with a run-up|You do the scary things, mostly, after a pep talk and some tensing up. That is what courage actually looks like from the inside. Notice what your pep talk says and write it down; you may want it for the next hard conversation.
B|17|20|You move through fear|You feel fear and act anyway, you learn from the misses, and you reach for the unfamiliar in small ways every day. That is a rare, quiet kind of bravery. Make sure it is aimed at things that matter to you, not just at proving something. Someone close to you may need to borrow a little of it.
Q|dreams-you-have-put-off|Dreams You Have Put Off|A check-in on the thing you have always meant to do, and the reasons it keeps waiting, for anyone with a dream that has been on hold for years.|Goals,Meaning,Motivation|2
P|The dream you have been putting off comes to mind…
O|4|Often, and it feels like an invitation
O|3|Now and then, with a wistful tug
O|2|Mostly at 2 a.m., with a heavy feeling
O|1|I try not to let it come to mind at all
P|What do you tell yourself about why it has not happened yet?
O|4|The timing was not right, and I am working on the timing
O|3|Life got busy, which is partly true
O|2|That I am not the kind of person who does that
O|1|That I never deserved it in the first place
P|In the past year, what concrete step have you taken toward it?
O|4|At least one real step, and I can name it
O|3|Some research, or a conversation about it
O|2|Thinking about it, which does not count
O|1|Nothing, and I have stopped believing I will
P|The thought “it is too late for that now” shows up…
O|4|Rarely, and I can answer it with examples
O|3|Sometimes, and I argue with it
O|2|Often, and it usually wins
O|1|As a settled fact
P|Who knows about this dream?
O|4|A few people, and they ask me about it
O|3|One person, who I told once
O|2|Nobody; saying it out loud feels risky
O|1|Nobody, and I have half forgotten it myself
B|0|8|The dream has gone quiet|You have pushed the dream so far back that you barely let yourself look at it, and the story you tell about why is unkind. That story is worth questioning. Write the dream down on a piece of paper, just the words, and put it somewhere you will see it. A therapist can help with the part that says you never deserved it.
B|9|12|Waiting for the right time|The dream is alive, and it keeps losing to life and to the voice that says it is too late. That voice is loud but not reliable. Tell one person about the dream this week, and ask them to check in on it next month. Being known changes things.
B|13|16|Moving toward it, slowly|You are taking real steps and answering the doubts when they come, even if progress is uneven. That is how most dreams actually get done. Schedule the next step now, with a date, before this feeling fades.
B|17|20|Living the dream, in progress|You are actively pursuing what you have wanted for years, you talk about it, and you have people cheering you on. That is momentum worth protecting. Watch for the point where the dream needs to change shape, and let it. Someone you know has a dream on hold; ask them about it.
